18 Dec 2023University of Washington, Department of Earth and Space Sciences

Machine-learning denoising exposes strong size scaling in deep-earthquake rupture parameters

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A multitask neural denoiser recovered teleseismic P-wave pulses from deep Mw 5.0+ earthquakes that had previously been too noisy for source analysis. The study found moment–duration scaling M₀ ∼ τ⁴, stress-drop scaling Δσ ∼ M₀⁰·²¹ and radiated-energy scaling Eᵣ ∼ M₀¹·²⁴; deep events had a median radiation efficiency near 5%, weak directivity and few subevents. After accounting for rigidity, no systematic depth trend in duration, stress drop or radiated energy remained across 100–700 km.

Technology: DenoTe, a transfer-learned multitask convolutional encoder–decoder neural network

Editorial relevance 53 physics Published articlePublished article Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Separated low-SNR teleseismic P-wave signals from overlapping noise, enabling source-parameter estimation from 920 deep-earthquake records, including moderate events ordinarily omitted from these analyses.
Test and result
A multitask neural denoiser recovered teleseismic P-wave pulses from deep Mw 5.0+ earthquakes that had previously been too noisy for source analysis.
Limitation / still unknown
The study is a source-parameter reanalysis enabled by neural denoising, not prospective earthquake prediction. It reports scaling relations and interpretations from the available global event sample; stress and energy estimates remain subject to waveform, path-correction and source-model uncertainties.

26 Jul 2023CMS Collaboration; CERN

CMS makes the first hadron-collider observation of boosted Z→cc̄ decay

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: In 138 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV proton-proton data, CMS observed boosted Z→cc̄ production in association with jets for the first time at a hadron collider. The measured signal strength relative to the Standard Model prediction is 1.00 with combined uncertainty +0.19/−0.17. A deep neural network (DeepDoubleX) identified large-radius jets from charm-quark pairs; the paper also sets a 95% confidence-level upper limit of 47 times the Standard Model prediction on boosted H→cc̄ production.

Technology: DeepDoubleX deep neural network for double-charm jet tagging

Editorial relevance 60 physics Published articlePublished article Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The neural network processed secondary-vertex and particle-flow candidate features to classify boosted two-prong jets as charm versus light flavor and charm versus bottom. Its charm tagging separated the Z→cc̄ signal from dominant multijet backgrounds and supported the first observation in the boosted high-transverse-momentum regime.
Test and result
In 138 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV proton-proton data, CMS observed boosted Z→cc̄ production in association with jets for the first time at a hadron collider.
Limitation / still unknown
The first-observation claim is specifically for Z→cc̄ in association with jets at high transverse momentum at a hadron collider; it is not the first observation of Z decay generally. The neural tagger is an important analysis tool, while the observation derives from the full CMS event selection and likelihood fit. In the same paper, boosted H→cc̄ was not observed; only an upper limit is reported.

12 Jun 2023ATLAS Collaboration; CERN

ATLAS graph neural network enables first observation of four-top-quark production

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Using 140 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV proton–proton collisions, ATLAS observed simultaneous production of four top quarks in same-sign dilepton and multilepton events, with 6.1σ observed (4.3σ expected) significance. The measured cross-section is 22.5⁺⁶·⁶₋₅·₅ fb, consistent with the Standard Model prediction within 1.8σ. A graph neural network separated the four-top signal from dominant backgrounds in the signal extraction.

Technology: Event-level graph neural network built with graph_nets/TensorFlow

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The GNN discriminant classified events using a fully connected graph of reconstructed jets, leptons and missing transverse momentum, with node, edge and global features. It separated four-top signal from background and supplied the discriminant used in the signal extraction and cross-section fit.
Test and result
Using 140 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV proton–proton collisions, ATLAS observed simultaneous production of four top quarks in same-sign dilepton and multilepton events, with 6.1σ observed (4.3σ expected) significance.
Limitation / still unknown
This is an ATLAS observation from its own 140 fb⁻¹ analysis after earlier ATLAS/CMS evidence; it is one paper-level observation, not a separate count for CMS or later combinations. The measured cross-section uncertainty is large and its central value is 1.8σ above the SM prediction, so this is not evidence of a significant deviation from the Standard Model. 1 May 2023University of Science and Technology of China; Mengcheng National Geophysical Observatory

Machine-learning classification shows rigidity-corrected moment release is similar across earthquake depths

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Random-forest classification of source-time-function characteristics for 3,675 global Mw > 5.5 earthquakes showed that depth-dependent rigidity explains much of the differences in rupture duration, length and stress drop between shallow, intermediate and deep earthquakes. Once medium rigidity is corrected, the analyzed events exhibit similar moment-release processes, supporting a constant-strain-drop interpretation across depths and host-rock types.

4 Feb 2023Miami University; University of Kansas

Unsupervised machine learning reveals previously missed hydraulic-fracturing seismicity in Live Oak County

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: An unsupervised repeating-signal detector, combined with template matching, exposed small earthquake bursts in a region of the Eagle Ford shale where conventional catalogs had detected little seismicity. The authors report that the new events temporally coincided with hydraulic-fracturing activity, and their regional analysis found detectable seismicity began after injection flux exceeded about two million barrels per month despite similar cumulative injection volume; this supports, but does not prove, a causal role for injection rate and is limited by sparse station coverage.

Technology: Repeating Signal Detector (RSD), an unsupervised machine-learning waveform-family detector

Editorial relevance 47 physics Published articlePublished article Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
RSD searched continuous seismograms without relying on preselected earthquake templates, clustered similar signals, and revealed small-magnitude event bursts missed by the previous catalog. Researchers manually inspected signals and analyzed their timing relative to hydraulic-fracturing records.
Test and result
An unsupervised repeating-signal detector, combined with template matching, exposed small earthquake bursts in a region of the Eagle Ford shale where conventional catalogs had detected little seismicity.
Limitation / still unknown
The RSD detections were manually inspected and some event locations were poorly constrained; the authors note station density limits detection and do not establish causation from temporal correlation alone. This paper extends an earlier Eagle Ford study but reports a distinct Live Oak County seismicity region and subsequent time-period catalog.

14 Dec 2022Columbia University; ETH Zurich; Swiss Federal Institute for Forest, Snow and Landscape Research (WSL)

Unsupervised seismic clustering reveals distinct drainage signals at Gornergletscher

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: An unsupervised analysis of five weeks of seismic records from Gornergletscher, Switzerland, separated cryoseismic signals into clusters associated with the 2007 Gornersee lake flood and with afternoon/evening meltwater flow. One high-energy, broadband noise cluster began about two days before the reported onset of subglacial flooding; the authors suggest it captures the earlier initiation of englacial and subglacial drainage and improves the flood chronology.

Technology: SpecUFEx unsupervised feature extraction: non-negative matrix factorization, hidden Markov modeling, principal component analysis, and k-means clustering

Editorial relevance 47 physics Published articlePublished article Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The unsupervised learning pipeline reduced seismic spectrograms to fingerprints and grouped similar continuous-noise and icequake signals without labels. Comparing the learned clusters with lake level, GPS, temperature, and event timing exposed distinct clusters associated with lake-flood drainage and daily meltwater flow, which supported the revised drainage chronology.
Test and result
An unsupervised analysis of five weeks of seismic records from Gornergletscher, Switzerland, separated cryoseismic signals into clusters associated with the 2007 Gornersee lake flood and with afternoon/evening meltwater flow.
Limitation / still unknown
The analysis covers five weeks at one glacier using the 2007 flood record. The inferred drainage start precedes lake-level evidence by about two days, but the source cannot definitively exclude ice-bed slip, till deformation, or small fractures as explanations for some spectral features. The study interprets cluster timing and spectra as hydrologic signals; it does not directly image subglacial drainage pathways.

7 Nov 2022Oak Ridge National Laboratory; University of Tennessee, Knoxville

Autonomous microscopy discovers an edge plasmon in layered MnPS₃

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: Deep-kernel-learning-guided STEM-EELS measurements identified a low-energy edge-localized plasmon response in the layered antiferromagnet MnPS₃, distinct from the higher-energy bulk plasmon response. The system used local structure to predict spectra and uncertainty, then autonomously selected where to measure based on a physics-defined objective. After 10–25 spectra, about 1% of the image-space points, it localized the edge mode to the flake boundary.

Technology: Deep kernel learning (feed-forward neural-network feature embedding with a Gaussian-process kernel and physics-based acquisition/scalarizer)

Editorial relevance 53 physics Published articlePreprint Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The model learned a structure-to-electron-energy-loss-spectrum relationship from sequential measurements and used its predicted response and uncertainty to choose subsequent STEM probe locations. A user-specified spectral scalarizer prioritized a low-energy feature relative to the bulk plasmon; the closed-loop instrument then discovered and mapped an edge-localized response without pretraining on MnPS₃ sample data.
Test and result
Deep-kernel-learning-guided STEM-EELS measurements identified a low-energy edge-localized plasmon response in the layered antiferromagnet MnPS₃, distinct from the higher-energy bulk plasmon response.
Limitation / still unknown
The paper demonstrates the mode in the reported exfoliated MnPS₃ flake and STEM-EELS setup. The physical search objective was supplied by researchers (a spectral feature below the bulk-plasmon peak), so the AI did not independently formulate the scientific question. The authors report that the edge response varied with local boundary shape and present this as a proof-of-concept discovery workflow, not a complete theory of MnPS₃ plasmon behavior.

4 Aug 2022ATLAS Collaboration; CERN

ATLAS boosted decision trees enable first observation of triple-W boson production

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: In 139 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV LHC data, ATLAS observed simultaneous WWW production at 8.0σ (5.4σ expected) and measured an inclusive cross-section of 820 ± 100(stat) ± 80(syst) fb. The analysis selected same-sign dilepton-plus-jets and trilepton final states. Two boosted decision trees—one per channel—improved signal/background separation and supplied distributions for the fit. The measured rate is about 2.6σ above the Standard Model prediction, not a significant discrepancy.

Technology: Two boosted decision tree classifiers for the dilepton and trilepton channels

Editorial relevance 60 physics Published articlePreprint Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: direct test. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The BDTs were trained separately for the two- and three-lepton channels, using 12 and 11 well-modelled event variables respectively, to discriminate WWW signal from background. Their output distributions entered the likelihood fit; the paper says the improved separation helped enable the observation.
Test and result
In 139 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV LHC data, ATLAS observed simultaneous WWW production at 8.0σ (5.4σ expected) and measured an inclusive cross-section of 820 ± 100(stat) ± 80(syst) fb.
Limitation / still unknown
The final peer-reviewed paper reports 8.0σ (5.4σ expected), superseding the earlier 2021 conference-note/press account that quoted 8.2σ and 850 fb. CMS had previously observed combined inclusive production of three massive gauge bosons; this ATLAS paper is the first observation specifically of WWW production. The measured cross-section is about 2.6σ above the theory prediction, below a significant deviation, with data-driven background and systematic uncertainties included.

10 Nov 2021Los Alamos National Laboratory; École Normale Supérieure; University of California, Santa Cruz and collaborators

Deep learning extends the mapped extent of a North Anatolian Fault slow-slip event

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A deep convolutional autoencoder trained on synthetic deformation and atmospheric-noise time series recovered millimeter-scale ground motion from real InSAR without being given the fault location. Applied to COSMO-SkyMed observations of the 2013 North Anatolian Fault slow-slip event, it detected about 2 mm of slip beyond the previously identified segment and revised the estimated along-strike event length from about 10 km to 15–20 km. The same paper used the approach to resolve transient millimeter-scale subsidence at California’s Coso geothermal field. This is one paper-level finding enabled by the model’s extraction of deformation signals from noisy satellite data.

Technology: Deep convolutional autoencoder trained on synthetic InSAR time series

Editorial relevance 47 physics PreprintPublished article Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The deep autoencoder separated persistent ground deformation patterns from atmospheric noise in real InSAR data without prior fault-location information, resolving the additional 2 mm slow-slip segment; the model was trained on synthetic data and was not fine-tuned on the real case.
Test and result
A deep convolutional autoencoder trained on synthetic deformation and atmospheric-noise time series recovered millimeter-scale ground motion from real InSAR without being given the fault location.
Limitation / still unknown
The newly resolved extension is a reanalysis of an existing 2013 InSAR event, not a newly occurring earthquake. The paper’s reported extension is model-derived; the original expert analysis had detected the larger slow-slip segment, while the autoencoder identifies an additional lower-amplitude portion. The model assumes atmospheric noise characteristics represented by its synthetic training data and omits some noise sources, including ionospheric perturbations. Coso results are a separate application within the same paper and are not counted separately.

26 Aug 2021University of Toronto; Vector Institute; Griffith University; Freie Universität Berlin

THESEUS derives resource-efficient designs for high-dimensional photonic GHZ and heralded Bell states

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The interpretable THESEUS design algorithm produced a three-dimensional six-photon GHZ-state blueprint that suppresses unwanted state terms toward arbitrarily high fidelity, with lower count rates, and a heralded three-dimensional Bell-state blueprint using destructive interference to cancel vacuum contributions. The same graph-based reasoning generalized the constructions to larger photon numbers or dimensions.

Technology: THESEUS

Editorial relevance 53 physics Published articlePublished article Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The authors’ AI-enabled automated design algorithm used an interpretable weighted, colored-graph representation of photonic experiments, gradient-based optimization, and topology reduction to search open experiment-design problems and expose generalizable physical concepts. The authors identify the work as explainable AI; the contribution is the algorithmically found design/concept, not merely faster benchmarking.
Test and result
The interpretable THESEUS design algorithm produced a three-dimensional six-photon GHZ-state blueprint that suppresses unwanted state terms toward arbitrarily high fidelity, with lower count rates, and a heralded three-dimensional Bell-state blueprint using destructive interference to cancel vacuum contributions.
Limitation / still unknown
These are theoretically calculated experiment blueprints, not experimentally demonstrated states in this paper. For the six-photon GHZ design, unwanted amplitudes can be reduced arbitrarily by adjusting graph weights, at the cost of lower count rates; feasibility is argued for contemporary photonics. The heralded Bell design’s estimated count rate and fidelity are modeled, not measured.

2 Jul 2021Université Grenoble Alpes; CNRS

Single-station CNN analysis adds 273 visually confirmed earthquakes to the L’Aquila near-fault catalog

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A two-stage 1D CNN pipeline applied to continuous three-component seismic waveforms from one L’Aquila station identified 273 previously uncataloged local earthquake events during a one-month 2009 foreshock period. The study reports that 84% were assigned magnitude below 1 and that all 273 new detections were visually confirmed; the expanded catalog contained roughly three times as many new detections as the pre-existing catalog had events for that period.

Technology: Two-stage one-dimensional convolutional neural network (CNN) pipeline

Editorial relevance 47 physics Published articlePublished article Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The detector CNN scanned continuous raw three-component waveform windows for earthquake signals; a second CNN classified distance and magnitude, enabling the researchers to find and characterize uncataloged events from a single station. The CNN pipeline generated the new event detections; authors then visually checked them.
Test and result
A two-stage 1D CNN pipeline applied to continuous three-component seismic waveforms from one L’Aquila station identified 273 previously uncataloged local earthquake events during a one-month 2009 foreshock period.
Limitation / still unknown
The analysis covers one month in February 2009 from one station. The authors report 273 new local detections and six nonlocal events among 369 windows classified as local; all 273 new detections were visually confirmed. The pipeline does not provide exact event locations, and transfer to other regions was not established. Magnitudes are coarse classes, not precise estimates.

EQTransformer expands the earthquake catalog for the 2000 Tottori sequence

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: An attention-based neural detector and phase picker reanalyzed five weeks of seismic recordings from the 2000 western Tottori earthquake sequence. Using 18 stations, the study detected and located 21,092 events, compared with 8521 in an earlier manually picked catalog using 57 stations. The inferred catalog included smaller events and sparse seismicity beneath the eastern flank of Mount Daisen. These are retrospective source-reported detections and locations, not individually independently confirmed new earthquakes or a forecasting result.

16 Jul 2020University of Auckland; University of Otago

Machine learning identifies seismic tremor precursors to Whakaari eruptions

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: A structured machine-learning analysis of nine years of Whakaari seismic records identified four-hour energy bursts that occurred hours to days before most of the five major eruptive episodes studied. The authors interpret the pattern as possible charging of the vent hydrothermal system by hot magmatic fluids. In cross-validation, the prototype alert model gave advance warning for four of five unseen eruption instances, including at least four hours before the 2019 eruption.

Technology: Structured machine-learning pipeline using engineered seismic time-series features, a random forest, and an alert decision model

Editorial relevance 47 physics Published articlePublished article Suggest a correction

Publication: Peer-reviewed paper · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
The machine-learning pipeline analyzed continuous seismic tremor data, selected precursor patterns, and modeled short-term eruption alerts. Its feature analysis identified the four-hour energy-burst pattern; the proposed hydrothermal charging mechanism is the authors’ geophysical interpretation, not a direct measurement of subsurface fluid movement.
Test and result
A structured machine-learning analysis of nine years of Whakaari seismic records identified four-hour energy bursts that occurred hours to days before most of the five major eruptive episodes studied.
Limitation / still unknown
This is a study of five major eruptive episodes at one volcano. The 4/5 warning result is cross-validation on this historical record, not independent operational validation; the model was described as a prototype. The ensemble alert score is not a calibrated eruption probability, and alert thresholds trade missed events against false alarms. The proposed hydrothermal charging mechanism is an interpretation of the seismic pattern.

6 Apr 2020DeepMind

Graph networks to understand glass dynamics

Source check: Source checked. Not independent scientific confirmation.

Finding: The networks predict particle mobility in simulated glasses; perturbing the network reveals growing spatial correlations as the glass transition is approached. Evidence comes from simulations: the learned correlation length does not translate directly into a measurable physical quantity.

Technology: Graph neural networks

Editorial relevance 53 physics Blog postScientific article Suggest a correction

Publication: Publication status unspecified in annotation · Test context: retrospective or benchmark. Catalog categories, not an evidence grade.

AI contribution
Graph neural networks predicted particle mobility and probed spatial correlations by perturbing particles in shells around a marked particle.
Test and result
Networks trained on simulated glasses predicted particle mobility and were checked against simulation ground truth, outperforming physics-inspired and state-of-the-art baselines (96% correlation at short timescales, 64% at relaxation time).
Limitation / still unknown
The network's correlation length cannot be directly converted into a measurable physical quantity; it remains an open debate; the work models simulated glasses, not physical samples.
